ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u1	Dear colleagues and colleagues! We continue the 7th regular session. Today's meetings cannot be attended by the following colleagues: Borut Pahor, mag. Dejan Židan and Roman Jakič. Nicely greeted! We're moving to five. At the end of the day, that is to say, a bill on amendments to the International Protection Act after a reduced procedure. The proposal was submitted by the Government to the National Assembly. In order to further explain the bill, I now give the floor to the State Secretary of Mags. Robert Marolt. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u2	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! The proposed amendment to the Law on International Protection is intended to supplement it by transposing an optional provision of Directive 2005/85/EC, which would establish a basis for determining the age of unaccompanied minors in the case of home age. There are several reasons for the abolition of free legal aid at first stage. The main reasons on which the proposal to limit free legal assistance and the support of refugee advisers in judicial proceedings before the Court of Justice and the Supreme Court of Slovenia in international protection proceedings are, above all, existing projects already carried out by NGOs and co-financed by the European Refugee and Budget Fund of the Republic of Slovenia. It is also important that non-governmental organisations remain present in an asylum home and continue to provide legal advice and information to applicants, which has so far proved to be very good and efficient or more appropriate than through additional refugee advisers. Finally, the reason for this proposal is also in the public financial situation in our country. The Law on International Protection is also being amended and supplemented in order to clarify the interdepartmental powers in the implementation of certain provisions and the existing legal text of the relevant editorial correction. The amendment introduces the possibility of establishing a medical examination to determine the age of a unaccompanied minor. The proposal for a solution follows the provisions of the Procedural Directive, which lays down in Article 17, paragraph 5, the procedure for determining the age of a unaccompanied minor. Since 2010 there has been a significant increase in the number of applicants for international protection who claim to be unaccompanied minors and do not have any documents to confirm their age, their age, and their age, the procedure for determining the age of unaccompanied minors has been introduced. The proposal for a new law therefore introduces a procedure for determining the age of applicants who claim, at the time of submission, that minors are unaccompanied, which is introduced by all other Member States of the European Union other than Slovenia and Greece. It is to be noted that the age-setting procedure by medical examination will be determined only and exclusively in cases where there is doubt as to the actual age of the applicant. At the same time, we stress that this proposal does not set out a concrete way, a specific method of determining age, but merely provides for a medical examination. By what method and by what standards age will be determined is the matter of an expert and his ethics, i.e. exclusively at the professional level, with the consent of both the minor and his legal representative to carry out the procedure. The amendments adopted in the Home Affairs Committee include some open questions from NGOs and some comments from the Legislative and Legal Service of the National Assembly. Dear Members and honourable Members! We propose that you adopt the relevant amendment to the Law on International Protection. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u3	Thank you. The draft law was considered by the Committee on Home Affairs as the working body of the mother. The rapporteurs or presidents of Mrs Maša Kociper, as I see, are not in the Chamber, so we will skip it. The following is a presentation of the views of the groups of Members. First of all, Mr Ivan Hršak has the floor on behalf of the DeSUS Group.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u4	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Good-bye, everybody! I will present the position of the DeSUS Group on the Proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on International Protection. Children without a escort are one of the most vulnerable groups in Europe, as they have also left their home country deprived of assistance, protection and protection by parents or other guardians. The Republic of Slovenia is a transit country for most unaccompanied children who leave soon after arrival and continue their journey to the countries of Northern and Western Europe. The reasons why they leave their country are different: because of the great poverty and the lack, the inability to find work and to study, military conflicts, persecution, life threats and other reasons. The arrivals of unaccompanied minors from third countries are not a temporary phenomenon but a long-term feature of migration flows in the European Union. Last year, 358 people applied for international protection in Slovenia and 104 for international protection in the first 6 months of this year. Since mid-2010 in the Republic of Slovenia, there has been an increase in the number of applicants for international protection who claim to be unaccompanied minors. The proposed Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on International Protection establishes a mechanism to determine the actual age of applicants for international protection who claim to be minors. In the DeSUS Group, we believe that the proposed solution is good and suitable for the situation, because in the vast majority of applicants there is doubt about their actual age, and it is particularly problematic that only few possess documentary evidence to prove their personal data. In addition, Slovenia is one of the few Member States of the European Union that so far did not know the possibility of a review order to determine the age of the unaccompanied minor in case of doubt at his actual age. The reasons why adults claim to be under 18 are obvious. Unaccompanied minors are classified as vulnerable categories of persons in need of adequate reception, care and treatment and are also assigned a legal representative. In practice, this has so far meant that adults have also been placed in accommodations intended for unaccompanied minors, and that there has been a greater possibility of damaging the development and safety of those applicants who are actually unaccompanied minors and the risk of trafficking in human beings. In addition, it is not possible to overlook the fact that the age of unaccompanied applicants can also affect the mere substantive decision on an application for international protection. While the bill abolishes the right to free legal aid and the support of the Refugee Adviser in the first stage proceedings. The reason for the abolition of free legal aid is substantial – the state’s state of public finances while at the same time providing legal assistance to applicants for international protection to NGOs who are present at the asylum home every day, we believe that their situation will not deteriorate. In the DeSUS Group, we will support the bill. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u5	On behalf of the New Slovenia Group, Mrs Iva Dimic has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u6	Thank you for your word. Your Honor! Under the Convention on the Status of Refugees, a person who has left the territory of his country of origin shall be deemed to be a refugee, for reasons of legitimate fear that, due to his race, religion, nationality, belonging to a particular social group or certain political belief, he or she is not in a position to be protected by his or her country of origin. A person like that needs and deserves protection. In accordance with this Convention, the Republic of Slovenia also offers protection to these persons under the International Protection Act. These persons are granted a number of rights, such as information needed to facilitate the inclusion in Slovenian society, accommodation in accommodation centres or in an asylum home, cash compensation for private accommodation, healthcare, Slovenian language courses, integration assistance programmes, single state aid and could also be listed. The Law on International Protection has so far provided all applicants with free legal assistance throughout the protection procedure, i.e. when the applicant submits an application for international protection in the presence of an appropriate translator or interpreter. Slovenia does not currently have a greater influx of refugees than in the 1990s, when the refugee problem was particularly acute due to the war in Bosnia and Herzegovina. In recent years, between 200 and 300 applications have been submitted annually, while there are barely 10 % or less favourably resolved requests. The majority of the applications submitted were therefore rejected, despite the fact that the applicants had regulated free legal aid during the whole procedure. The narrowing of the right to free legal aid is so logical and, on the other hand, these forms of legal aid will still be granted to those applicants who are actually entitled to refugee status or international protection and will enforce such status in proceedings before the courts. We stress that international organisations, in particular the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees and NGOs, play a major role in addressing refugee issues. In Slovenia, the Jesuit Refugee Association plays an important role in this field of refugee issues. This organisation has been providing assistance to all refugees for the past 10 years, regardless of race and religion, with particular emphasis on assistance to vulnerable groups – especially children without adult escorts. However, abuse is taking place in this area. In recent years, there has been a doubling of applicants without personal documents and without accompanying adults who claim to be minors and, in fact, this is not the case, since they often turn out to be over 30 years of age in the proceedings. In order to determine the age of applicants, it is therefore necessary to cooperate with experts in the medical profession. Our legislation has not been regulated so far and it is therefore necessary to amend the law. However, the legislator's task is to regulate the legal basis for preventing such abuses in a way that other European countries have incorporated such safeguards into the system. By amending this law, we will continue to maintain an adequate level of rights of refugees with international protection in accordance with international conventions and European directives and allow for the acquisition of the legal status of refugees or persons with international protection to those who are actually entitled to this right. Members of the New Slovenia Group will be supported by the Proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on International Protection.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u8	Thank you very much. The Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on International Protection is controversial for Positive Slovenia at three points. Delete the possibility of submitting an application for international protection at the diplomatic and consular offices of Slovenia in third countries. In general, we are opposed to proposals that will reduce rights for the weak, and even in one point we are opening wide opportunities for abuses and violations of fundamental human rights. It is particularly worrying that the drafter of the law on the committee responsible did not present any valid argument for such a change. When the legal aid is lifted at first instance, it is intended to be one of the reasons for saving. At least that's how it was originally explained. Calculations based on the assumption that 350 asylum seekers would be asked for assistance do not, of course, hold, as the figures show that the number of asylum seekers per year is falling and that this number is actually a phantom. On a concrete question why the Government is in fact abolishing aid at the first stage, we have received the answer that this is a substantive reason. This task is expected to be performed perfectly by NGOs, explained the State Secretary. We cannot accept such substantive reasons, as NGOs are competing for funding in individual projects when the project is over, and the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service of the National Assembly, which the coalition has obviously deliberately overlooked when I summarize just a few warnings. [[...]] The continuous amendment of legislation on free legal aid is controversial in terms of equality before the law. One marriage introduced it, the other took it again, the third not, the novel gave it, and now we're shutting it down again in an extremely short time. This shows that, in fact, there are no reasonable reasons for such acts, and it is also stated in the Constitutional Court's decision that this is a matter of extremely complex and complex legal proceedings, especially for minors. It is a particularly sensitive group and vulnerable group, not only among applicants, but of course at all, and our Constitution provides special constitutional security and protection for children. Finally, asylum seekers are generally ill-educated people, and justice is, as it were, complicated. While, at the second stage, where the Government retains free legal aid, they no longer have the right to propose new evidence and facts. Free legal aid is therefore particularly meaningful at the first stage. Medical examinations of persons suspected of not being minors, although they claim otherwise, are particularly problematic. We will talk more about this in the debate, but let me point out that our concerns are both health and legal. We don't know what methods the doctors are supposed to use. In some European countries, doctors have even rejected this finding. They therefore rejected the task of carrying out the so-called age survey. At the same time, methods are unreliable. However, for invasive methods such as X-rays, a person must give consent, which is not valid if we do not inform the person of what such an examination means to him first and what the consequences may be. Such reviews interfere with human physical and mental integrity, which is once again a constitutional category. The proposed articles are therefore not adequately defined, as the Office of the Ombudsman also pointed out. Therefore, in Positive Slovenia we strongly reject the proposed law. Its purpose, as it appears, and as we have heard on the committee responsible, is not clear or proposed, it has overlooked the warnings of the Legislative and Legal Service, and at some points it is even dangerously approaching to cause constitutional interference with the rights guaranteed, and most importantly, it denies assistance to the most vulnerable at the level that is most important to them. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u9	On behalf of the Slovenian Democratic Party Group, Mr Ivan Pišek has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u10	Thank you, President. A nice welcome to you all, Secretary of State, Members and Members of Parliament! The Government has proposed to the National Assembly that due to less difficult changes following a rapid procedure, it should consider the proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on International Protection. The proposed material is intended to supplement the law by transposing an optional provision of Directive 2005/85 to determine the age of unaccompanied minors in the event of doubt at the actual age. As a result of the public financial situation, it is also proposed to abolish free legal aid and support for refugee advisers in the first stage proceedings. It is also proposed to delete the possibility of filing an application for international protection at the diplomatic and consular offices of the Republic of Slovenia in third countries, thereby depriving the diplomatic consular network of its competence in third countries. Furthermore, given the experience of the existing provisions in practice, it is not possible to implement them at all. The proposal for amendments to the introduction of age-setting procedures for unaccompanied minors introduces this amendment because, since 2010, there has been a significant increase in the number of applicants for international protection who claim to be unaccompanied minors and do not have any documents to confirm their age, so this definition of age by medical examination is introduced. In Article 15, a unaccompanied minor shall be classified as vulnerable persons with special needs who are provided with special care, care and treatment. In view of the guarantees given to unaccompanied minors as a vulnerable category of persons, the situation of such a minor brings various benefits and additional obligations compared to the treatment of full-year applicants for international protection. Given that, in practice, in most cases, there is doubt as to the actual age of the applicant, since these applicants are already acting older than they claim, so far such applicants have been considered minors in the absence of adequate levers to determine the actual age. This resulted in the greatest damage to actual minors, as adults who merely claimed to be minors were also accommodated in accommodations for unaccompanied minors. In view of the above, as unaccompanied minors, the principles of the child’s greatest benefit are also addressed. The proposal therefore introduces a procedure for determining the age of applicants who claim that minors are unaccompanied upon application. It is to be noted, however, that the age-setting procedure by medical examination will be determined only in cases where there is doubt as to the actual age. A medical examination to determine the age of an alleged unaccompanied minor may be carried out only with the consent of the minor and his legal representative. If the minor and his legal representative refuse a medical examination for no reasonable reason, the unaccompanied minor shall be treated as a full-year-old. Health reasons will be considered in particular as grounds for rejecting the medical examination. It should also be pointed out that the application of a person who has applied as a unaccompanied minor and refused a medical examination should not be rejected solely because of this fact. With the amendment to abolish the possibility of filing an application for international protection at diplomatic and consular offices in third countries, it is proposed to delete the possibility of submitting an application to the DKPs of the Republic of Slovenia in third countries, thereby depriving the competence of the diplomatic and consular network. Moreover, given the experience of the existing provisions, it is not even possible to implement them now. The possibility of submission is purely a national practice which does not have a basis in other EU legislative acts, so the applicant has also examined the possibility of submitting an application for international protection in the DKPs in a comparative manner and has found that these options are not provided in their legislation, for example, by Germany, Finland, the United Kingdom and the Czech Republic. Sweden, for example, has it, but such requests are automatically rejected. Statistics show that in 2005 there was no request, no such procedure. In 2006, four were launched in Skopje, one in Zagreb, one in Zagreb, one in Zagreb, 2009 was really seven, 2010 was none, 2011 was one and also this year none. This also in practice proves that there is no demand for such aid and that training of employees at diplomatic and consular offices is unnecessary. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we will confirm the amendment of the law on international protection.

Every minute, eight people around the world must leave everything behind to escape war, persecution, and intimidation. Many refugees must decide between bad and worse. Slovenia, as a signatory to the Geneva Convention and a member of the European Union, is obliged to provide international protection to such individuals. According to a survey by the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees for Central Europe, Slovenia has significantly fewer asylum seekers than any other European country, as well as a minimum percentage of recognised refugee status. Most asylum seekers seeking safe havens come from Afghanistan, Georgia, Iraq, Pakistan, Serbia and Turkey. Slovenia has already provided such persons with an effective asylum system. An asylum system that operates quickly and effectively, which is fair and allows any asylum applicant who really needs international protection to be granted refugee status. Such a system also prevents those who do not need such protection from benefiting from the system. That is to say, Slovenia has shown this since the war in Bosnia and Herzegovina, when we hosted up to 60,000 refugees. With the proposed amendments to the amendment of the Law on International Protection, we follow the Directive of the European Union and introduce the grounds for determining the age of unaccompanied minors in case of doubt in the actual age. In addition, we are introducing some other changes that have proved necessary in practice. The proposed abolition of the right to free legal aid and the support of first-degree asylum advisers is questionable. It is in this area that non-governmental organisations implementing a number of aid programmes are very helpful to the country. Among them is the Legal Information Centre, which provides legal advice and information to international protection seekers as well as to persons who are granted protection. With his permanent office he is also present at the Asylum House in Ljubljana, as well as within individual projects at the border and at the Centre for Foreigners in Postojna. In the context of its activities, it shall provide assistance and advice to applicants on the crossing of the State border and also on the possibility of submitting a request for protection or for an effective and fair administrative procedure. As many as 80% of all beneficiaries or applicants benefit from their legal aid and are satisfied with it. And that help remains. It makes no sense for such aid to be financed from two bags in the future. Slovenia already has this area legally regulated and through its institutions it provides a high level of legal and social assistance to international protection seekers. We are confident that the proposed amendments will not affect the effectiveness of the procedure for granting international protection, so we will support the proposal.

And just the changes in the law on international protection are this step in this direction: to protect the weakest, no matter where they come from. In this case, too, there are certain interventions where the suspension of a medical examination means a loss of the possibility of obtaining a particular right. That's the right to work. However, there is no doubt that any intervention in the body is carried out only in extremely urgent cases where there is no other method, that the examination must be clearly described in advance and that physical and mental integrity must be fully respected in the course of the intervention. The principle of the child’s benefit must be the highest guidance in any decision we make, regardless of whose children are, and no matter where they come from. We will therefore support the amendment of the Law on International Protection in the Slovenian People's Party's Group. Thank you.

The method must be very clear when we are talking about it, very clearly defined and cannot be left to the individual's judgment. He can't and he can't! Especially not on the basis of physical characteristics. That's what I'm stressing. There were proposals for this review to change with some multidisciplinary approach. Not only are these checks of age, but other independent experts, psychologists, social workers and the like are included in this. So, let's say this opinion, if you think there has to be and there's a tremendous amount of tax money being lost at this point, maybe it makes sense to use at least that kind of thing. And let me say this is also the position of the Council of Europe, the Union of European Ombudsmen and the European Association of Paediatrics, who even urge pediatricians not to carry out such checks, because it has been proven, once again, to be proven, that is not something that I would say is very unreliable and the results can go back up to a few years. ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u48	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. I have listened with interest to this debate about Article 6 of the Law. Let me explain again. In the European Union, all Member States, with the exception of Slovenia and Greece, have an age-setting procedure for unaccompanied minors. That actually says a lot. Why? The essence of the proposed amendment to Article 6 of the Law is to prevent abuses that occur in an increasing number or to attempt abuses, as Mrs Magis warned. Waste, which is now existing legislation, does not give any legal basis to prevent such abuses, to determine the actual situation when an unaccompanied applicant claims to be a minor. Look, there's no legal basis for this. According to experts, abuses are taking place, attempts to abuse, but this country cannot now be checked because it does not have this mechanism. Therefore, this amendment to the law is proposed to test the determination in case of doubt as to his actual age. The age test or the age determination procedure will be conducted exclusively by the profession, exclusively the medical profession, the forensic experts. This will not be decided by officials, nor will it be decided by politicians, not by the judiciary, but by the profession. And the medical profession is thrown away by its medical regulations, other acts of law, doctors also have Hippocrates' oath, there will also be judicial experts who are legally sworn experts, and let us make it possible for the profession to do so. Why? I'll give you a very practical example. We have an applicant who claims to be underage and comes to Slovenia for international protection. Since Slovenia cannot now verify or determine its age, it is in doubt that it is a minor and is recognised as a minor. Then this person goes to another country of the European Union, for example, to the neighbouring state of Austria, Italy, Germany or anywhere else in the European Union. And this other country, when it comes to their territory, will check whether this person is underage or not or how old he is. This country shall then, in accordance with their national legislation, in the context of this procedure, find before the competent physicians that a person is of full age and is not a minor. And what happens? This country shall immediately return this person, in accordance with the Dublin Agreement, to the Republic of Slovenia. And the black mark falls on our country, why it does not determine the actual state. The actual situation must be identified in all types of procedures, whether administrative, judicial or other proceedings. And what happens? This person is returned to Slovenia. How do you treat her now? We have information from another country of the European Union that a person is of age. And according to our regulations, he has the status of a minor. But it turns out she's not underage. For this reason, this procedure is now in place. As regards methods, procedures, methods, I would like to point out that as soon as the amendment of the law in the government was adopted, we have obtained initiatives from NGOs, the initiative of the Paediatric Clinic, and in this context the amendment of the coalition was also prepared and adopted on the parent committee. As far as I'm concerned, once again, the procedure at the doctor's office, it's a professional thing. Let the profession know, let the profession decide. This novelty of this article of the law allows the exercise of the constitutional right and the rights of the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the greatest benefit of the child. This is a fundamental principle that binds our country and we must not forget it. Now what's happening is that we have one of the applicants who claimed to be underage. The state does not have procedures and levers to verify, but is, in fact, full-time. And what happens? He is considered a minor and may even be located in the same premises as a minor. See, this is not in line with the principle of the greatest benefit of the child. This has a negative impact on those who are actually children who are actually asylum seekers as minors and there is also a risk of trafficking in human beings. Therefore, these anomalies, which are taking place, unfortunately, in practice, must be eliminated. As far as this medical examination is concerned, I must stress this. A medical examination to determine the age of a suspected unaccompanied minor will only be possible with the consent of a unaccompanied minor and his legal representative. If both of you refuse this medical examination, the person who claims to be a unaccompanied minor will continue to be treated as a full-year-old. So if age detection is disabled. I am sure that that child and his representative, who will know that the child is actually a child, that he is a minor, will certainly let his actual age be determined in case of doubt. Those speculators, those who know they're not underage, they'll probably react differently. That is why this provision is in line with the principle of the greatest benefit of the child and the prevention of abuse. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u62	Thank you for the floor, Madam Vice-President. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! The purpose of the new law on police tasks and powers is to prepare a modern European comparable and transparent law, which will systematically and comprehensively regulate one place and set out police tasks, general police powers and enforcement resources, and will also regulate a quality and effective procedure for resolving complaints against the police. In preparing the draft law, the international conventions on the protection of human rights, which bind the Republic of Slovenia and the recommendations of domestic and foreign institutions and organisations dealing with the protection of human rights in police procedures such as CAT, the UN Torture Committee, CPT, the Council of Europe Committee for the Prevention of Torture and Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment, and so on. The origins of the law were also internal and external expert analyses, research and projects, findings of controls, reports of the Ombudsman, important jurisprudence and decisions of the Constitutional Court and the European Court of Human Rights from Strasbourg, and of course the theory and practice of modern democratic police. The basic objective of the bill is to correct and supplement the provisions in the areas which have proved to be tightly and inexactly defined during the implementation of the current law on police, thereby ensuring an even higher level of legal and personal security of people and their property, and to bring them into line with other system laws. If I mention just a few major solutions, the law regulates police tasks and so-called security police powers. These are the ones the police are using to prevent danger. It is the fundamental source of police procedures and the most important police rule for preventive and repressive police action. It is a central but not the only source of standardization of police powers. Better supervision in the field of police powers is ensured, as their application is subject to more detailed and legal reporting and supervision, and a procedure to resolve complaints against the work of police officers is also laid down in more detail. The issue of sub-legal signatures is a novelty in the rules for the exercise of police powers. The draft law thus explicitly stipulates that the Minister shall issue a regulation on the exercise of police powers after obtaining the previous opinion of the Ombudsman. The latter represents a higher standard of human rights and freedoms, particularly in the part where police, with their powers, intervene in human rights and freedoms. The law also specifically exposes and sets out the principles for the use of police powers. The principles promote the highest standards of police work, respect for the rule of law and human rights in all police activities, confidence in the public in the work of the police, adequate systems of accountability for police work, understanding and openness of citizens to the police. Among the fundamental novelties of the bill are, if you allow me to mention only one thing: the substantive clarification of indeterminate legal terms, the definition of police procedure and tactical discretion, the new police authority, the roadblock. Criminal acquisition and evaluation of information is further defined. The new ones are police powers to ensure security and order at sports events, to prohibit participation in sports events and to suspend travel, to change the conditions for the use of a service dog, to change the conditions for the use of firearms, to establish a gap between temporary restrictions on movement and deprivation of liberty, to carry out police duties abroad and to act as foreign police officers in the Republic of Slovenia, to take account of the duration of the police procedure until the detention is determined during the detention period and to make the appeal procedure more clear and transparent. Thank you.

You know there are also complaints about police work, and these things are very clearly regulated in this law, that first things, disputes between citizens and police officers are trying to settle in a conciliation process, but if these matters are not successful in the conciliation process, they go ahead with the Senate. This is just one of the solutions that this system law brings. According to New Slovenia's assessment, it is a good law, which is why we predict support for it.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u69	Thank you very much. Parliamentary Group of the Slovenian Democratic Party and Mr Tomaž Lisec.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u70	Have a nice hello to you all. Dear Vice President, Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! The Police Act has been in force since 1998. During this time, it has been amended and supplemented several times, in accordance with the requirements of practice such as operational needs and the changed circumstances of police operations in recent years, the decisions of the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia and the legal order of the European Union, in particular the so-called Schengen acquis. 14 years is a long enough time when our legal system and society, and unfortunately also crime – in particular organised, economic and cross-border – have grown. Therefore, in the Member States, we support the thinking from the previous government that the whole so-called police regulation should be regulated by the adoption of two system laws in this area, which together form an effective whole. It is therefore imperative to adopt a system law on the tasks and powers of the police, as well as the law on the organisation and work of the police. This is complex legislation, as we are touching on the constitutional areas, which also affect human rights and fundamental freedoms, and in the implementation is now an extraordinary weight on the Police Powers Rules, i.e. the day-to-day exercise of the duties and powers of the police in various situations, including sports events, which has already been widely mentioned in this space and this mandate. The law also regulates very limited situations when successful intervention can be turned into a black chronicle. Police officers in the field can say that there is a narrow line between a hero and a fool, especially when it comes to security on the one hand and human rights interventions on the other. And as has been said several times by the SDS Group, this limit can also be decided in microseconds. The bill is not about jumping into the unknown, as the bill covers the complex content of police powers already used by the Slovenian police in carrying out police duties. Notwithstanding the fact that individual police tasks and powers are also dealt with in other procedural rules, in this law, for the first time in independent Slovenia, in a conceptual sense, they are more systematically processed, in particular general police powers, accompanied by a broad explanation of fundamental concepts, principles and other provisions. It is not my intention to repeat in the position of the SDS Group all the objectives, purposes and reasons that have led the government to the drafting of the law. The bill defines 29 police powers. This says enough about how difficult it is to prepare this law, especially since, as I have already mentioned today, we can, at every step, strike on the other hand to defend human rights and fundamental freedoms. In this presentation of the opinion of the SDS Group, allow me to highlight why, in addition to the systemic resolution of the subject, we support the proposal for a law which we believe can be further cleared through the hearing in the National Assembly. In the SDS, in particular, we would like to emphasise the appropriate comparison of the bill with European practices and the protection of human rights. It is important to point out that such regulation of police law, which is a provision in two laws, is primarily Anglo-Saxon. Thus, they have the same system of law in Great Britain, as well as Austria, Germany, Sweden, Australia and Croatia. Australia also has different arrangements across regions. It is also important to point out that the drafting of the bill has consistently taken into account the international conventions on the protection of human rights that bind our country and the recommendations of domestic and foreign institutions and organisations dealing with the protection of human rights in police procedures. In addition, it should be pointed out that the starting point in the drafting of the law was also internal and external analyses, research and projects, monitoring findings, reports by the Ombudsman, important case law and decisions of the Constitutional Court and the European Court of Human Rights. So the bill has been processed from all sides. It is also important to point out that a review of internal and external proposals was made prior to the drafting of the bill, and a review of the comparative regulation of police powers in Germany, Spain, Sweden and Croatia, as I have already mentioned, as well as the emphasis on individual police laws by Austria, Hungary, Serbia, Slovakia, the United States of America and so on. This is also beautifully presented in the drafting of the bill. There is also another human rights intervention, which we believe must be very specific in order to avoid problems in practice. In order to ensure human rights and fundamental freedoms, a number of guardians have been created by means of a proposal for a law that specifies the collection of notices and the recruitment of persons, regulates the identification, regulates in detail the security review and review of the person, removes the police authorisation of arrest, sets a distinction between constitutional categories of restrictions on movement and deprivation of liberty, specifies the rights of persons who are restricted or deprived of liberty, shortens the time allowed to intervene in human freedom, while also adding the time of the police procedure to the time of detention, which has been several times a matter of debate in the past. Procedures with vulnerable groups are also clearly regulated and in some cases the duration of detention is reduced. In practice, it is clear that there is a contradictory relationship between ensuring security and respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms. Ensuring security requires certain interventions into other human rights and freedoms, otherwise the absence of security would lead to a situation where human rights and freedoms could not be realized at all. Perhaps at this time of discussions on the financial stability of the country, it is irrelevant that the proposal does not introduce any new provisions which would have financial implications for the state budget. After a thorough review of the law, it is clear that it is a very thorough consideration of every specific task of the police or of every police officer and of all the powers that the police or every police officer must exercise in their daily encounter with people or with different situations. Let me get this straight. In the SDS Group, we believe that the draft law on the tasks and powers of the police is a very substantive proposal for a law that does not bring about revolutionary changes, but together with the Labour and Organising Act in the police will ensure that the police work is still at a higher level in all segments. In the SDS Group, we will therefore support it in the first reading, and in the further consideration we will be able to see all the well-founded proposals that will further improve the law. If we want a real state of law, i.e. a country to the extent of all and not just individuals, both laws must be adopted and it is essential to amend legislation in the field of the prosecution and the judiciary. But it's a topic for another time. Thank you.

In conflict situations, police are the first factor to step into the so-called crime scene and then everything happens in seconds. In my opinion, the essence of the law lies in Article 1 of the bill, and if you allow me to quote it: "Police carry out tasks and powers under the provisions of this law in order to ensure the basic duties of the police, which include: ensuring the security of individuals and communities, respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, and strengthening the rule of law." The proposal for a law provides for clearer and more transparent regulation by introducing basic concepts in the handling of police powers. For the first time in the history of Slovenia, a police procedure is defined and clear conditions are laid down for the use of general authorisation. For the first time, general principles for the performance of police tasks are laid down. More specifically, the performance of our officers' duties abroad and their functioning within the European Union are provided for. These tasks are significant, and it is also important that, in certain police powers, the solutions seek to improve the effectiveness of the police, which I believe is the task or effort of all of us to make the police as effective as possible in their work, and that is to say, in reducing all criminal or criminal offences in our country. The bill also defines the police's measures regarding the prohibition of approximation, since we have repeatedly had, at least in the social field, these cases, when we are repeatedly at some border, what does the restraining order even mean. More specifically, measures on domestic violence are introduced, new police powers are introduced to prevent violence at sports events, regulate the protection of certain persons and facilities, and define the collection and transmission of data more precisely. The draft law was submitted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for consideration by the assembly. With regard to this bill, a group of ten Members and Members with the first-signed Mr Jank Webro requested that the National Assembly hold a general debate. In order to further explain the proposal, I give the floor to the representative of the Government of Magi. Robert Marolt, Secretary of State of the Ministry of the Interior. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u132	Thank you for the floor, Mr Vice President. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! The aim of the draft law on police organisation and work is to regulate the normative basis for the modern police organisation, its management and direction, and the clear delimitation of powers between the ministry and the police, to regulate specific labour relations in the police, the status and protection of police officers, training, education and research activities, and to ensure the police's closer involvement in the local community and closer cooperation of police with other actors, thereby improving the organisation and effectiveness of police operations. The main solutions to the bill are as follows. For the first time, a precise, clear definition of the relationship between the Ministry and the police and the definition of the tasks performed by the Ministry for the police. Secondly, a more detailed definition of the direction and supervision of the Ministry of Police, the powers of the directors of the Directorate for Police and other security tasks of the Ministry and the restrictions on the exercise of control when the direction is taken over by the Prosecutor of State. Thirdly, strengthening decentralisation and autonomy and autonomy of police administrations. Compared to the current regulation, the draft law places particular emphasis on greater autonomy in the functioning of police administrations, thereby decentralising the police with clear strategic orientations and the starting points of the General Police Administration. The definition of the competences of individual organisational units shall take into account the principle of subsidiarity, which allows the right work to be carried out in the right place. In particular, this means that the General Police Administration carries out strategic tasks, with the exception of operational units such as the Special Unit, the Aviation Police Unit and certain other units; the Police Administration shall in particular carry out coordination tasks at regional and police stations directly with the tasks and powers of the police. Fourthly, a system of appointment of the Director-General of Police is being reintroduced, equivalent to the system of appointment of other Directors-Generals in the highest positions in public administration. Fifthly, public information on police work is also extended in the draft law to the directors of police administrations and commanders of district police stations, which inform the public about security events in their area. Six, police orientation towards the users of its services and effective cooperation with other bodies and institutions and entities. Here I must stress in particular that it is a matter of strengthening cooperation with the local community, establishing reporting obligations at the request of the municipal council and aligning the annual work plan of the district police station with the priorities of the local community. Thus, the so-called priority of modern police organisations, such as the Slovenian police, is directed towards police work in the community. The police organisation is intended for the users of services, i.e. the self-government local community. Week, enhanced partnership to ensure security by setting up councils, commissions' councils or other agreed forms of partnership. Eighthly, particular emphasis is placed on the specificity of the regulation of labour relations, which allows the smooth performance of operational tasks, the performance of tasks under special working conditions, overtime, temporary relocations and special emphasis and concern for employees, legal assistance and other forms. And the last. This is about upgrading the education system and gradually moving to a higher level of education to perform police duties for most of the police jobs. The bill on the organisation and work of the police forms a round whole with the proposal for a law on the tasks and powers of the police, which we have concluded in this regard. Both regulations will form the basis of police organisation and [[...]] [[a sign for the end of the debate.]] Thank you very much.

We agree with the above statements and with the provisions, so I also announce our support for the bill in the first reading. Now the position from the point of view of national communities. The draft law and the sub-legislative acts annexed to the proposal do not provide for the visible public and official use of the language of indigenous national communities in the national mixed area and the use of the symbols of indigenous national communities in those areas, which means that all the provisions relating to this are in force in accordance with other laws or regulations. However, since there are some inconsistencies that we are pointing to in this regard, let us also make our point or comment in this debate in the context of the first reading of the law. It should be noted that there is a need to ensure consistent bilinguality on the boards marking police administration facilities and police stations based in the national mixed area. And in particular to elevate the flag of the national communities along with the flag of the Republic of Slovenia and other prescribed symbols at police and police stations based in the national mixed area. Unfortunately, this is not always the case. Let me just illustrate that in the case of the national mixed area in Prekmur, the use of the symbols of the Hungarian national community is quite consistent, but negative cases despite our warnings, we are looking right at the police and judicial facilities, which is, to say the least, rather strange and also incomprehensible in view of the high standard of legal protection of the indigenous national community. In support of this, I would like to stress that the public and official use of the language of indigenous national communities in national mixed areas is one of the fundamental special rights of national communities which enables the existence and development of these communities. The special rights of the national communities include the use of the symbols of the national communities, which are used in those areas together with the symbols of the Republic of Slovenia and the European Union. This is defined, as we all know, in Article 11 and Article 64 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia, in the decisions of the Constitutional Court and also in the provisions of the sub-legislative acts governing both areas. So, once again, we will uphold the law, but I hope that these warnings will be heeded. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u135	Thank you very much. Mr Jerko Čehovin on behalf of the Group of Ambassadors, Positive Slovenia. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u136	Dear Members and Members, Honourable Vice-President, Secretary of State and other Members! Unfortunately, the bill on organisation and work of the police contains more concerns of Positive Slovenia than the law, whose first examination we have just completed. The disputed starting points, which included the first proposal of law, organisation and police work, have been corrected three times in interdepartmental coordination, which we are pleased to note in Positive Slovenia, as we have warned ourselves about these controversial solutions. With each correction, the Ministry has softened the disputed articles, and some unacceptable solutions remain in the bill. Thus, in Positive Slovenia, we cannot support the law because of the following provisions: the first time, the appointment and discharge of the Director-General of Police and the Director of the National Investigation Office, which, according to the draft law, are carried out under the Law on Public Servants, is Articles 48 and 5 of the bill. For we are advocates of an autonomous professional commission, which is a kind of protection against political recruitment and has been introduced into the process of election of the Director-General of Police by a novel of the Police Act. In our view, the advantage of this Commission is that the Minister's candidate can be refused, which means that the Minister cannot propose to the government and must find an alternative. However, a change in the manner in which the Director-General of Police is appointed, as proposed by law, represents a major step backwards in ensuring the independence of the operational functioning of the police. This allows the Minister of the Interior to be completely free of time with regard to the appointment of the Director-General of the Police. Even worse, the Director-General of Police no longer has any protection against the Minister's will, even when it comes to his dismissal. Secondly, the degradation of the National Investigation Office into the ordinary criminal police sector. As a starting point, the draft law takes into account the fact that the National Investigation Office of the Interior is an organisational unit of the Directorate of the Criminal Police of the General Police, specialised in the handling of individual crimes and in the organisational sense part of the Directorate of Criminal Police. Thus, the National Investigation Office becomes only one of the internal organisational units of the police administration, although the analyses have undeniably shown that the existing organisation of detection and prosecution bodies, as well as their competence, working methodology, strategies and priorities, are not systematically appropriate to respond to new complex forms of crime. That the National Investigation Office is losing its operational autonomy through a new arrangement, it is also shown through a provision on the manner in which the Director-General of the Police will appoint and dispose of it under the Public Servants Act. Following the current legislation, the appointment of the NPU Director shall also be subject to the same provisions as the appointment of the Director-General of Police and through the determination of who determines which investigations of suspected crimes will be carried out by the National Investigation Office – no longer the Director of the National Investigation Office but the Director-General of Police with an internal act on a proposal from the Director! From now on, the National Investigation Office will be subordinated to the politically appointed Director General of Police and consequently to the Minister. Our concerns about the abolition of the Autonomous Professional Commission have also been raised yesterday in the appointment and resolution of the Director of the National Investigation Office and have also been presented in practice and shown to be fully justified and absolutely nothing exaggerated. Thirdly, the laxity of the provision for the recruitment of the so-called auxiliary police, Article 103 of the bill. The current Police Act provides, inter alia, for the recruitment of auxiliary police to replace the involvement of a large number of active police officers in carrying out large security risk tasks. However, Article 103 of the Police Organization and Work Act provides for the recruitment of auxiliary police officers solely for the purpose of replacing the absence of a larger number of police officers. Such a diction thus enables the recruitment of auxiliary police officers, for example, in the case of a police strike, which is very controversial in our view. Even if we remember the spring statement by the police and the minister of the threat that there are a lot of people at the employment offices who would work for 600 euros. Such a loose conduct allows for the replacement of police officers who carry out legally defined forms of union struggle. Among other things, auxiliary police officers are generally not trained to carry out police tasks on their own, but they do so with active police officers and are more helpful to them and act on the orders of an active police officer, and thus, by carrying out police tasks on our own by auxiliary police officers, we cause a greater possibility of injury at work and a greater likelihood of overstepping police powers. There is also a question of whether the Minister of the Interior is plotting a kind of parallel police force. These are the main concerns of Positive Slovenia, which we hope will be eliminated during the legislative process, or that the provisions will be written down more clearly, so that we will conclude that the law is suitable for further consideration, support for the law as a whole, and make it conditional, in particular, on the requirement for an autonomous professional commission to appoint and resolve the Director-General of the Commission and the Director of the National Investigation Office. For the end of the day, just one more thought. The Republic of Slovenia is one of the rare countries which does not have a service to protect against foreign intelligence activities. Owl is exclusively an intelligence service. At present, it is, of course, more about protecting business or economic interests than typically national interests, but it is also not negligible, even more so. Some of the countries through which we like to imitate ourselves, such as in Germany, are part of the Ministry of Home Affairs’ counter-intelligence service. Given that we are talking not only about the organisation of the police today, but also about the Ministry of the Interior, as the proposal for a law at the Ministry of the Interior introduces a new internal organisational unit responsible for the direction and supervision, we are interested in whether you have considered any additional services in the Ministry of the Interior responsible for the anti-intelligence activity within the Ministry. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u138	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Secretary of State and distinguished colleagues and colleagues! The draft law on police organisation and work is also a twin law replacing the 1998 Police Act. In the SDS Group, we are aware that the proposal for a law on police organisation and work is one of the most important laws in the field of internal affairs. The law updates and regulates the functioning of the police. Given that police activity is primarily the activity of protecting people's lives and property and ensuring order, the tasks of the police are one of the vital parts of caring for people and ensuring national security, as we know that the police control national borders, which is the external border of the European Union today. The law responds to modern efforts to adapt the police to changing social conditions. The police must always be brought closer to the needs of the people and their safety, so they are more and more from a strictly hierarchical and centrally managed body in modern times [[Incomprehensible]] Which is also the intention of this marriage. The objectives of the bill are also based on these principles, which enable modern police organisation, governance and guidance by means of regulatory arrangements. For democratic political systems, it is particularly important to have a clear delimitation of powers between the Ministry and the police, as well as a very clear and limited direction and supervision of the Ministry of Police. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, we are aware that the Constitutional Court also intervened in this matter in 2003, and therefore we believe that the proposal for a law brings clear solutions, which have already been constitutionally and judicially verified. Of course, the restrictions applicable to the Ministry in the direction and management of police control are also important when the prosecutor takes over the direction of the police. The Minister already has certain powers under the Law on State Administration towards the body in the composition, the proposal for a law specifies the type of guidance that is considered to be guidance and how it is implemented. The powers necessary to carry out their tasks effectively are also laid down by the officers who supervise the work of the police. Articles 12 and 13 also set out the manner and extent of the notification of the Minister and the reporting of the police. The public has the highest interest in the recruitment of key police personnel. In the previous mandate, under the Ministry of Minister Kresalova, a rather unique system of appointing the Director-General of the Police has been introduced in relation to practice in public administration. The new law abolishes this specificity and the appointment of the Director-General of Police is equivalent to the appointment of other Directors-General. At the time of the establishment of the National Investigation Office, an unconventional mode of appointment and responsibilities of the Head of the National Investigation Office was also introduced, since the NPU Chairman was not hierarchically placed in the office and powers of the Director General of Police and the Director of the Criminal Police. The draft law also normalises and Europeanises the position of the National Investigation Office. In the parliamentary group, we are of course convinced that increasing the decentralisation and autonomy and autonomy of police administrations is the right direction and that the principle of subsidiarity allows rational division of tasks, if of course the strategic starting points of the General Police Administration are taken into account. We are aware that the number of uniformed police officers has decreased in recent years, and new solutions need to be sought to allow for a more rational use of human and material resources. The solution, which also allows for the relocation of other police and police personnel, including technical and professional public officials, to provide police support for the smooth performance of police tasks, is one of the steps in this direction and we support such guidance in the parliamentary group. As the discussion in the previous twin law has shown, it is clear that the Government has prepared this law with great care to give careful consideration to the positive solutions and importance of this matter for the situation in society and the country. Therefore, in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, we assess that the solutions in this proposal are positive and we will support the bill on organisation and work in the police. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u141	Thank you very much. Mr Richard Braniselj on behalf of the Parliamentary Group of the Civil Gazette. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u142	Thank you, Vice President. Slovenian police are the organ of sui generis, the organ of a special type and is the largest authority of the state administration in the Republic of Slovenia. Its specific organisational and functional status is also a situation requiring the search for optimal forms of internal organisation and regulation of labour relations. The draft law on the organisation and work of the police replaces and builds on the existing Police Act in that part relating to the normative basis for the modern police organisation, its management and direction and the clear delimitation of powers between the Ministry of Internal Affairs and the police, the regulation of specific labour relations in the police, the status and protection of the police officer, training and education. In particular, it is essential that the proposal of the law restores the greater role of the local community in setting priorities, pre-drafting the annual police station work plan and reporting the police station's command on security relations in the local community. This will certainly contribute to greater police cooperation with the local community, thereby improving public security and reducing fear of crime and consequently improving the effectiveness of police work. In a specific case, this means that the law restores certain relations that have already been in law in the area of the Police Act, but that law has not been regulated in recent times. It is also important that the law makes it possible to differentiate the different jobs of police work. In this way, within five years of the entry into force of the law, the Ministry will systemize those particularly demanding positions of operative police officers requiring the rank of higher professional education. This will provide the basis for the most exposed, key and capable operatives to be better ranked and better paid. There is no change in the appointment and discharge of police station commanders and police chiefs, and the appropriate candidates for the director of the police are checked, as in all cases of public administration recruitment, by the official council. The National Investigation Office shall maintain the position and specific status within the police by making the circle clearer or more specific; the appointment of the Director of the NPU shall be comparable to the appointment of the Director of Police. The draft law is mainly inter-resortaryly coordinated, and especially for two years, it has also been coordinated with the interested public, the internal and the external, since the material was first published on 13 December 2010. The Parliamentary Group considers that the proposal for a law on police organisation and work is appropriate for further consideration in the National Assembly.

A worse solution is certainly the current regime, when the director of police is virtually irreplaceable. This does not mean that the police should be subordinated or dependent on the government or the minister, but certainly the competent minister, who is objectively responsible for the work of the police, must have the opportunity to appoint a director of a person who is judged to be the professional and responsible leader of the police's work for the benefit and security of everyone in the territory of the Republic of Slovenia. In the Slovenian People's Party's Group, we estimate that the proposed changes represent a more optimal organisation of the police, which will be able to perform its function more effectively and more responsible for the greater benefit and security of citizens and citizens. ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u145	Thank you very much. Mr Ivan Hršak on behalf of the Group of the Democratic Party of Pensioners of Slovenia.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u146	Dear Mr Vice-President, distinguished Secretary of State, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! I will present the position of the DeSUS Group on the draft law on police organisation and work. The proposal for a new law on police organisation and work is the second of both laws under the current Police Act. Thus, according to the current Police Law, the Minister of the Interior may seek a candidate for the Director-General of the Police through a public or internal call or call, and before submitting a proposal to the Government, the Minister is obliged to obtain the opinion of the Special Autonomous Professional Commission on the suitability of the candidate. However, the draft law on police organisation and work provides that the Director-General of Police shall appoint and dismiss the Government on a proposal from the Minister. The provisions of the law governing the system of public servants shall apply to the procedure. Under this law, the public tender shall be conducted by a special competitive commission appointed by the official council. The proposal reintroduces the system that was in force until 2009 and is already applicable to the appointment of other Directors-General. At this point and at the point of redefinition of the National Investigation Office, Members of the DeSUS Group expect the most public debate on this bill. The Special Autonomous Expert Commission will also no longer be required to appoint a Director of the National Investigation Office. The Director of the National Investigation Office will appoint and resolve the Director-General of Police. The current law provides for the National Investigation Office to operate outside the criminal police, and this bill puts it within the criminal police framework, to which it belongs. The task of the National Investigation Office will no longer be determined by itself as it has been before, but will be determined by the Director-General of Police. Otherwise, the basic objective of the bill is the high level of legal and personal security of people, the security of their property, the strengthening of the rule of law, the strengthening of cooperation with the local community and the decentralisation of the police. In doing so, the text of the law follows the objectives set out in the Coalition Treaty. One of the novelties of the law is also the provision of greater autonomy and autonomy for police administrations. To this end, the power to decide on the right to legal aid paid is transferred to the directors of police administrations. The proposal also further limits powers between the ministry and the police, and the Minister will not be able to direct the work of the police when the prosecution takes over. The innovation leading to a more rational functioning of the body is a long-term development programme for the smooth functioning of the police, which will enable its sustainable development. This is a strategic document that contains different areas, such as personnel, equipment, education and the like. In the DeSUS Group, we hope that this document will enable strategic planning of resources and labour capacities that will enable police to work smoothly and qualitatively, with equipment that will not be sustained and lacking, thereby also increasing the level of satisfaction with the work among the police themselves. At the same time, we expect this document to contribute to the development of new methods, tactics and police work techniques. The police will submit each year a report on their work to the Minister, who will be forwarded to the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for information. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia will provide it to the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia. We also expect the DeSUS Group to contribute to strengthening the decentralisation and autonomy and autonomy of police administrations. We also hope that the bill will actually increase the police's orientation towards the users of its services and enable effective cooperation with other bodies and institutions and entities. In doing so, we focus on strengthening cooperation with the local community, which is expected to be achieved by aligning the annual work plan of the district police station with the priorities of the local community and by strengthening partnership in providing security through the establishment of councils, councils, commissions or other agreed forms of partnership. The DeSUS Group estimates that the proposal for a law brings good solutions that will help the more effective work of the Slovenian police, so we will support the conclusion that the bill is suitable for further consideration.
