ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u152	Thank you very much. First of all, let me make it very clear that I am in favour of this law and that our Group of Members also supports it. However, in our parliamentary group we always have ceterum censeo, which our leader also stresses – namely, that one is law and the other is implementation – and that the actual good work of the police depends mainly on good governance, on directors, on the leaders of individual organisational units and on police and criminals who carry out concrete work. I think that this law on police organisation is a nice opportunity for a little wider discussion of what we expect from Slovenian police, in fact, what we want from it, and I think that the opinion of our people, citizens and citizens is united here. On the one hand, we expect the police to scrupulously investigate and suppress crime, especially the part of crime that is most consumed, which devours Slovenian society in a moral sense, in a valuable sense, and creates some impression of injustice in society. We continue to hear that it is unacceptable, and people are right that it is unacceptable, on the one hand, to lower rights in different areas, because we need to reduce public spending and on the other hand, some eccentric crime cases remain uncleaned, do not get epilogues in court. So, the prosecution of crime is extremely important, and I would think that way about the NPU here. On the one hand, the NPU would give recognition and praise for what was done because certain things were done. There was the first, although not yet a legal, judicial judgment in the Merkur case, which could be some trait that had not yet brought the ointment, or a ray of hope, at the end of the tunnel. Here the NPU has to admit that this difficult case has apparently been very well investigated, prepared good evidence, worked well with the prosecution, and the case has also ended well in court. There have also been other cases that the NPU has investigated and are still pending before the courts or the prosecution: the NKBM case, the Krško Nuclear Power Plant Fund, the Hippo case, the Thaler case, the TŠ 6, the Koper Municipality, etc. So a lot of work has been done, and that's why the NPU is doing the best it can. Of course, work needs to be improved. Economic crime and corruption need to be pursued even more decisively. It is necessary to work even more professionally and cooperation between the prosecution and the police, the NPU and other segments of the criminal police should be even better. In doing so, I would stress that this work of investigating crimes is a very difficult, professional work, it is not something simple, it is a work that – especially when cases are difficult – takes quite a while. What we need in this society is not a media spectacle, of which we will have nothing, from media-covered arrests we have nothing. We need the well-done work of the NPU and the criminal police at all, we need strong evidence to equip the prosecutions for being able to defend the case well, to require punishment, to require sanctions and then to bring matters to a high-quality conclusion, with quality judgements in court. It is not necessarily a measure of the success of the criminal police, how many convictions come from the indictments, because the level of level at which an act must be proved is different for the initiation of an investigation, for the prosecution and for the final conviction. Thus, we must not always say that the police have done a bad job if in all cases it does not end with a conviction; however, the crime in this case has not been proven. And what is a criminal offence can ultimately be decided only by the court; not by politicians, not by Members, not by the media, and none but by the courts. The second thing we expect from the police is public order and security. Here I would particularly warn and say that the police will be successful if people in this country feel safe. In doing so, I would like to point out that sometimes, unfortunately, some police leaders have a bureaucratic attitude towards public order and security and that they do not understand the mission of the police, because the mission of the police is that we are safe. It is not the mission of the police to punish as many offences as possible, to write as many fines as possible, to punish as many times as possible, to warn, etc. The police will be successful if we are safe if there are few traffic accidents, if there is a little violation of public order and peace. What do I mean by that? If this is clear, if this mission is understood by the chiefs, if understood by the Director-General of the Police, if understood by the Chief of Police, then all police officers will be directed to this and will not put themselves at those points where people are most likely to commit formal offences, because they drive 5 kilometres beyond the limit, but accidents in that area are not, there are no negative phenomena, but there will be very many penalties – in short, if they work on the standard, so much and so many penalties each month, and you will get a positive assessment of the performance of work – but the police will be successful if they are on the ground where the neurological points are, where accidents occur; where the victims are, where there is material damage; where there are violations of public order and peace. The police must be there, even though they punish less and bring less fines into the state budget. So, the bureaucratic approach goes away and I would say "fisco-ocratic" is that the successful police are the one that brings as much money into the budget as possible. This is a bureaucratic way of thinking, even some mayors sometimes think this way and it's wrong, it's not the sense of the authorities that care about public order and security. Years ago, as a minister, I participated in a conference on quality in the public sector and there the Swedish police presented their example of developing a quality model. So the quality models that are at home in the economy, in companies, in the private sector, are also applied in some countries in the police. Sounds amazing, but that's right. The Slovenian police also have some attempts this way. There the measure of success was developed quite differently than I think it is happening to us. Whether one police station director, one police station commander is successful or not, in Sweden depends on how people feel safe in the local environment, whether people feel safe or not. If they feel safe, then the police are successful and the director of the police department or the commander of the police station is highly valued and is also well-known for his salary. With regard to the law, I must say that the Civil Code has been very strongly and intensively involved in the coordination and preparation of this law, or I will not say exactly when it is being prepared, but in the coalition coordination phase, we have made quite a few comments and I must say very frankly that these comments have been taken into account. So, all praise from our ministry, Minister Gorenak, Secretary of State, Secretary of State on the team. In short, we are satisfied, some things with which we were not satisfied in the first draft of the law were amended, supplemented and now the law is fine in our opinion. The appointment of the Director-General shall be arranged as is the case with the other Heads of State Administration, i.e. top management in the State Administration, in accordance with the Public Servants Act. And that's right! It is a two-phase procedure, in the first phase the filter is professional, so through this filter only top competent candidates can come, even the conditions in the law are very sharp, and in the second phase the minister can choose the capable and professional and competent candidate with whom he can cooperate and who will trust him. I have known the new Director-General of the Police since I myself, as my colleague Janja Klasinc, worked for the Minister of the Interior, and I must congratulate Minister Gorenak on his election as Police Director-General. The new Police Director-General is a professional and police officer from head to toe; therefore, I have no fear that this man would dare to influence himself from politics in order to hope to give himself commands, instructions on what to investigate and what not. So I have great confidence, because the functioning of systems depends most on people, more than on marriage. It is more important to me than what this law on police will be, that Stanislav Veneiger becomes the general director of the police. The NPU retains certain autonomy, which is excellent, well, I strongly support and say in this work that they have done so far, they must now upgrade and be that pillar and that point and that institution in which people will have confidence that they are really working to prosecute economic crime and corruption. Of course, we have also taken a good look at wage matters, and we like the fact that the law is keeping, so that it does not interfere with the single public sector wage system. Every time legislation changes, either police or military, or tax or any other, attempts are made to dissipate a single public sector payroll system. I know because for years I've been working on how hard it is to keep such a system together and how dangerous it is for you to get one piece out of this system. Then he starts tearing himself up like a domino, and it's not possible to stop. Despite the fact that this payroll system – so-called Virantov – is in fact Drnovškov-Ropov-Bohinčev-Janšev-Virantov – is said to be a project of several successive governments – although it is quite a convenient excuse for many things, this payroll system is the one that allowed us to enter the crisis with a significantly lower mass for wages than otherwise. In order to clarify this, it should always take ten, fifteen minutes, but it is clear to me and my acquaintances: if after 2002, the wage stampede we had from 1994 to 2002 would have been about 15% higher at the beginning of the public sector wage crisis. I can prove that to anyone at any time. That's why I'm defending this single salary system because it's a good tool and it's a guarantee that wages don't go up uncontrolled, and it's good that the Police Act doesn't crash it. One little thing I'd like to point out, maybe one more thought before the second reading, there's another possibility, an opportunity for some kind of amendment. At one point, I think things could be improved. It was years ago that logistics services, human resources, finance, accounting and so on were moved from the police to the Ministry of the Interior. In my opinion, this move was poor, the police is a large system, it has its own management autonomy, it has its own director-general, it is independent and logical that this Director-General also supports professional services in the field of personnel, finance, accounting and so on, which are in his hands, which can order things from them and which he professionally supports. It is illogical that the chairman of one such strong and independent body must turn to the Ministry for professional support for his managerial decisions. And imagine, the same staff service for the Ministry, which is significantly smaller by number of employees, and for even the police, which is significantly larger by number of employees. And that's the problem. Since then, ministers have had a completely incomprehensible ambition for me to bring these services to the Ministry, I don't know why, I don't know, to reduce autonomy in this way, to have things more under control, I don't know; but it's not practical, it's not smart, it's not rational, and it certainly makes it harder for the police to function. So think about another treatment here, if it is possible to fix it. I'm concluding. The Civil Code supports the law. It expects further improvements to the second reading and stresses that it is mainly far more dependent on leaders, criminals and police officers. Much more than the law depends on Stanislav Veniger and his team on the police. I firmly believe that Slovenian police can be trusted that this is a quality, trustworthy institution, but as in other public sector institutions, there is always room for improvement.

The relationship between the Ministry and the police is aimed at the second chapter of the law. In my opinion, when I read this provision, there is nothing at stake; only some of these are certain entertainments, which we will not, in practice, detect, because the law provides nicely for the police to be a body within the Ministry and that the Minister has certain powers under the State Administration Act in relation to the body in the composition. The bill also sets out more specifically the type of guidance — such as basic, annual and individual orientations — which is considered to be targeted at all and how it is implemented. According to the law, the direction of the police means a systematic and planned action of guidelines and mandatory guidelines for the performance of police tasks with the aim of fulfilling the basic mission of the police and being implemented through written guidelines and instructions issued by the Minister. The minister has also stressed this many times in his performances, but apparently he has stumbled upon a number of deaf ears and is trying to involve him in some stories that are not there. Why? Because the bill makes it clear that the Minister's powers do not apply to police procedures whose orientation is taken by the competent state prosecutor on the basis of the law governing criminal proceedings. It further makes it very clear that, irrespective of the law governing criminal proceedings and the law governing cooperation between the police and the prosecution, it is considered that the State Prosecutor has taken over the direction of police work in the pre-trial procedure from the moment he was informed of the crime. That is how many times the Minister's involvement in the work of the police ends. It is also very clear when the Minister can assign control. He may appoint a supervisor and other officials. It is also important when the Director-General of Police informs the Minister of any events relevant to the safety of life or protected persons or facilities, when he has to report to him on events in which several persons have lost their lives or material damage has occurred. Why am I talking about this? Because I have a feeling that, in the previous term, this story was actually happening more at some semi-family level than at some professional level. So, I welcome these intentions of the bill's proponents that these matters are very clearly defined in the law. The Minister may request reports, data and other documents relating to the performance of police work. Here I think the matter is crystal clear and you don't need some stories. With regard to the independence of the police or the new director's appointment, thank God we get this law! ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u186	Thank you for the floor, Madam Vice-President. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! I thank you for this very interesting debate that I have listened to very carefully. I will try to answer some of your opinions, your opinions, your questions, and then the legislative procedure will continue. Initially, as regards the harmonisation of this law with trade unions. The point was made if the law in certain articles or individual articles was not in line with the unions. Here, if I may, I would explain to you that the so-called agreement between the police unions and the Government of the Republic of Slovenia of 30 May this year does not provide that police legislation will be harmonised or does not specify that it must be harmonised. This agreement provides in Article 18 that the Government of the Republic of Slovenia will not propose these two laws without social dialogue and seeking agreement with the unions. Here I must immediately explain to you that we have had a very correct, very professional social dialogue with the trade unions, which is also clear from the views of the police unions which have been published publicly on their websites, as well as from other publicly available documents. The social dialogue took place and there was also a search for consensus. Of course, the search for consensus does not mean agreement or agreement, but rather an effort to achieve as much as possible, to achieve consensus as possible. Of course, the search for consensus does not mean and cannot be reached a consensus purely 100%, to the last point and to the last comma. The Police Union of Slovenia was exposed. It is true that the Police Union of Slovenia did not take part in the last call for coordination, but of course we also responded to all their initiatives and proposals on the way and on an up-to-date basis. This was how social dialogue took place and there was also a search for consensus. I can also tell you in particular, with representatives of both unions, coordination meetings were held already on 11 June and 13 and 20 August, this last coordination meeting on 20 August, the representatives of the Police Union of Slovenia did not attend, while the other union had no problems and attended this meeting. That's how the government here, the ministry, they've done their job. The National Investigation Office has been exposed. Allow me to explain to you that the second paragraph of Article 21 of the Law on the Organisation and Work of the Police stipulates that the National Investigation Office is autonomous in the performance of its tasks. This amendment seeks to ensure an appropriate organisational, legislative, material and human resources framework for the long-term professional and efficient and, above all, the operational autonomous functioning of law enforcement and detection bodies. This proposed arrangement allows experts from different fields to be involved in the handling of cases at all stages of the hearing. In this way, multi-disciplinary research is ensured and only in this way can effective, up-to-date and quality work be ensured. The main objective of this Office or such a way of working is the specialisation of crime detection and investigation bodies, which means that the National Investigation Office specializes in those crimes that are most specific in Slovenia, in particular economic, financial, public financial and cybercrime and corruption. So from areas that are most at risk, as has already been exposed, social and economic regulation and the foundation of the country, and follow all technological innovations. The NPU will, with the knowledge of specific experts and appropriate technical and appropriate information support and – which is very important – through permanent education and training, provide more effective detection and investigation of such crime. As regards the appointment of the Director of the National Investigation Office. As already mentioned by the distinguished Mr President of the National Assembly, Dr Gregor Virant, this law is the fruit and result of quality coalition coordination of the law and a number of legal solutions have been improved by the intensive participation of members of the coalition. The Director of the National Investigation Office will be fully public and transparent and will be chosen in a transparent manner in accordance with the Public Servants Act. Thus, a call will be issued, the procedure will run through the Official Council, the Official Council will appoint a special three-member commission, the special three-member commission will carry out a selection procedure between the candidates applied for and the Director-General of the Police will then decide who she will appoint as director. It is therefore a system-wide arrangement, as is the case for other highest positions in the public administration. As far as the National Investigation Office is concerned, I think this is the most optimal solution, so that real work can be carried out smoothly, effectively and with all available human resources and financial resources and material resources. Here I would also like to point out that a Council for Police Law and Powers, composed of eminent internal and external experts, is also established in the Ministry of the Interior or in the police department, dealing with various professional issues and dilemmas relating to the application of police powers or police law. This is very important because it has also been exposed to these powers, which are newly regulated in this law or certain powers that have already been completed, are now being adequately developed. I fully agree with the President of the National Assembly, who said that, on the one hand, legislation is only a legal basis that lays down the conditions for the work of police or police officers, criminals and other employees, and on the other hand, it is an effective management, an effective internal organisational structure and, at the end of the effective work of individual police officers and criminals. As far as the targeted research project is concerned, I would like to point out here that this targeted research project on measuring the effectiveness of police work is about to be completed, which will finally offer an optimal model for managing and organising police work on the ground. This will be duly accepted in the near future. As far as the appointment of the Director-General of Police is concerned, the same conditions are laid down in the law as for the second highest position on official posts in the public administration or public sector. Here the law provides for an additional condition that 15 years of work experience, not anywhere in the state administration, but only in the police. So that he will be appointed Director General of Police and Director of the National Investigation Office exclusively as a career policeman. As regards the placement of the National Investigation Office, the National Investigation Office is now, with this bill, organizationally more clearly integrated into the Criminal Police Office. And once again, the National Investigation Office will be completely autonomous in its work, just like the whole police. The question of the auxiliary police was also raised. Allow me to explain to you here that union freedom is already a constitutional category and the law already clearly determines when police officers must carry out their duties even at the time of the strike. So, police officers already have a limited right to strike in marriage, and there is any fear of calling the auxiliary police, completely out of line. The financial implications of the new police legislation have also been highlighted. The financial implications are indeed valued at EUR 50 000 500, but they are for the payment of daily compensation for injured police officers, to which police officers have not been justified so far. This is also one of the consequences of the strike agreement between the Government and the police unions. A question or concern was also raised that certain requirements of police unions relating to salaries and other rights from the employment and service relationship should also be included in the articles. Here I would stress what Mr President of the National Assembly has already said that we do not break this law and cannot break the single wage system that applies to the entire public sector. With regard to the issue raised or the membership of police officers in the political party. Here we know that the Constitution already prohibits membership in political parties, both for policemen and for professional members of the defence forces. The law now clearly provides for sanctions in Article 61, namely, police powers are withdrawn from the police, and not only are they suspended, as previously laid down. So, here we are redefining membership in political parties because it is a constitutional category, but only sanctions are imposed for infringements. Thank you for now.

Today, in the debate on the organisation and work of the police, we are discussing an extremely important area, an area which interferes with the functioning of the so-called repressive authorities. Following this standard classification, the police and the military are among the repressive authorities. The military is losing importance in modern times and is no longer such an important factor in the political context. That's why the role of the police is so much more important. Not only does the police ensure security and order and so on, the rest of the tasks that we are familiar with, but, in some way, with the prosecution and the judiciary, it can also have a very comprehensive and profound impact on human rights. In the past, these relationships were relatively simple: on the one hand, there was a strong country, sometimes a church that had great power, which accused, condemned and burned people who did or did not do anything wrong in one person. With the development of democracy, these relations have changed significantly and have changed so that responsibility for individual areas and for the protection of human rights provided by constitutions and different conventions has been divided into different bodies. In Positive Slovenia, it seems to us that another very important development is taking place in the context of the economic crisis; an event that can go deep into our fundamental statehood and the foundations of our democracy. Something important is happening in the field of so-called repressive organs and law enforcement. Robert Marolt, Secretary of State at the Ministry of the Interior.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u196	Thank you for the floor, Madam Vice-President. Following the completion of the implementation of the Resolution on the National Crime Prevention and Suppression Programme for the period 2007-2011, the Interdepartmental Working Group, which brought together 21 representatives of ministries and representatives of two academic institutions, drew up a resolution for a new period. With the new resolution, we continue our efforts to maintain the commitments and standards already adopted in the field of security policy and build on the resolution in force so far. The main objective of the Resolution on the National Programme for the Prevention and Suppression of Crime for the period 2012-2016 is to ensure permanent and long-term security for all people in Slovenia. The fundamental principle of the resolution is based on preventive action and not repression. As a result of this fact, the police are not the only body dealing with crime, so all those stakeholders who can in any way help prevent crime in specific areas are covered as the holders of individual tasks in the resolution. The resolution highlights 13 areas of crime prevention and control, namely property, economic, cyber, organised crime and the fight against terrorism; the area of illicit drugs, juvenile violence, domestic violence, assistance to victims of crime; the area of enforcement of criminal sanctions; environmental protection; and the prevention of environmental crime. The resolution also gives particular emphasis to the confiscation of property benefits and defines the concept of intelligence-led police activity. In all these phenomena of crime, we must not forget the fear of crime. It is important that people feel safe, which is also the fundamental objective of this resolution. To coordinate and oversee the implementation of the resolution, it is envisaged to establish an inter-ministerial working group composed of experts from various ministries and other bodies working in the field of crime prevention and control. In order to carry out the individual tasks set out in the resolution, no specific financial resources are planned, but will be planned within the framework of a programme-oriented budget for individual resources. We will also draw the bulk of the funds from the European funds. In the course of its consideration of the motion for a resolution, amendments were adopted on the home committee, which mainly followed the comments of the Legislative and Legal Service and are editorial in nature. In addition, an amendment of a substantive nature was adopted on the home committee in section 6.11.4.2 of the Strategy/programme — Monitoring the implementation of the Law on the Seizure of Property of Unlawful Origin. The chapter has changed so that it now reads: Taking away the property of an illegal source. The main change is that by the end of December of this year, an analysis of the implementation of the Law on the Seizure of Property of Unlawful Origin is prepared and, by the end of January, the following year, the necessary amendments to the law with the aim of significantly increasing efficiency in this area. Ladies and gentlemen, I propose that you adopt the resolution on the national crime prevention and control programme in question for the period 2012-2016. Thank you very much.

The most important thing is in this Law on Criminal Procedure. An extremely complex law that changes so often that neither experts, prosecutors nor judges can monitor these changes and then respond effectively and internalise them and implement them in practice. Only when someone internalises a legislative change will it take some time to implement it, will this change in practice come to life and can have positive effects. The constant change in the organisation is also linked to legislative changes in the process. One such case is the previously mentioned change in the organisation of the NPU. In this context, it makes sense that the police authorities, such as the police director, then the prosecution authorities, such as the Attorney General, and the judiciary have some mandates, which are not partly covered by each other. Otherwise, there is a situation such as now that, within each mandate, all management personnel on these bodies are replaced and then a truly one political party or political power controls this whole area of action. And that's what we sometimes say a little bit easily: a police state or a repressive state. The meaning of these diverse mandates, which are sometimes five, sometimes six years, is precisely in this combination of individual political currents for another, that the director from one political area is in the police and the prosecutor from another. This enables this cooperation and co-control. Then there is a condition for quality work. We want all these bodies to work more and more effectively while at the same time giving them less and less money, including others, offices and others. Resolution on the national crime prevention and control programme 2012-2016 The document, which, on the one hand, exposes the situation of our society, on the other hand, draws attention to those segments that we need to pay particular attention to and require our in-depth treatment, and shows how we work to deal successfully with the problems that exist. Criminality is not itself, its appearance in certain forms and segments is due to the fact that, somewhere and in a certain way, our country and society do not operate in a broader sense as they should, for such or other reasons. Monitoring, analysing and addressing different forms of crime over a period of time is a framework for those areas that we will devote most of our efforts to over the next four years and that we will continue to talk about a successful fight against crime after the end of this resolution when we adopt a new one. The resolution on the national programme for the prevention and control of crime for the period 2012-2016 focuses on thirteen areas which we can narrow down into several sentences. As a result of the economic crisis and the reduced income available, wealth and economic crime have increased, which are becoming increasingly violent, resourceful, carried out in an organised society, and are often entangled in an international dimension. They were joined by illicit drugs and cybercrime as an extremely lucrative criminal activity. Particular attention will be paid to victims of crime, intelligence-led police activities and the enforcement of criminal sanctions and the confiscation of property benefits. At the same time, we must not forget the highly subjective element of crime, the fear of crime itself. At this point, I would like to stop at something that we would like Members of the Slovenian People's Party to stress in particular. The analysis, which is the basis for the resolution, not only shows us the consequences of the fact that we are in the economic and social crisis, but also points out that we are also in a crisis of values. In such a deep crisis of values, we must also face plans to address juvenile violence, violence against the elderly and domestic violence in the country's strategic paper on the fight against crime. These are very sensitive areas because they interfere in the most intimate and private spheres of human life, in areas where each of us should feel the safest and most protected. In the Slovenian People's Party's Group, we therefore support guidelines for more effective prevention in addition to effective prosecution of crime in the resolution. We are convinced that, above all, an effective preventive positive family and demographic policy is the country's. As also stated in our Constitution, it is the duty of the State to adopt measures that support and create conditions for parents to have the opportunity to provide and raise their children and young people in a way that will have the most important first and best example in their parents and regulated family life for the lives of values, mutual respect and patient conflict resolution. Security, such a beautiful word. But security isn't just about giving. Security is a good thing that we have to fight for every day, every single one of us, regardless of the position that someone is in, and no matter where we come from. Globalisation, unfortunately, brings a lot of negative things into our lives in addition to positive things. Only by working together to find solutions to address the social causes of crime will our fight be more successful. Members of the Slovenian People's Party are aware of their responsibilities in this area, so we will support the resolution on the national programme, which is the first step towards preventing and suppressing crime in the next period. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u209	Thank you. Parliamentary Group of the Democratic Party of Pensioners of Slovenia and Mr Ivan Hršak.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-10-25-SDZ6-Redna-07.ana.u210	Thank you for your word, Madam Vice President. Dear Secretary of State, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! I will present the position of the DeSUS Group on the Resolution on the National Programme for the Prevention and Suppression of Crime for the period 2012-2016. Slovenia is a relatively safe country, although according to the data of the last decade, the number of reported crimes is increasing. The growing problem is mainly the increase in violent crime, the lowering of the age limit of juvenile offenders, the classical and new forms of organised crime, the modern forms of economic crime and the functioning of foreign criminal associations both across and within the territory of the Republic of Slovenia. The motion for a resolution before us seeks to ensure that the people and residents of the Republic of Slovenia are provided with a safe environment, thus enabling quality of life. The proposal covers 13 areas of crime prevention and control, with the DeSUS Group focusing on the area of property and economic crime, taking away property benefits, organised crime, environmental protection, juvenile violence and domestic violence. In the DeSUS Group, we would like to draw further attention to violence against older persons in the context of domestic violence. For the profession points out that this is a serious problem, especially in times of crisis, when violence against the elderly has been proven more. The number of people who are more vulnerable and exposed to abuse is also increasing with demographic changes, as the number of older populations is steadily increasing. At the proposal of our Group of Members, an additional text was added to the Committee on the Interior on the protection of the elderly. We believe that it is right to pay particular attention to children and young people, but we must be aware of the fact that the elderly are part of the vulnerable category. Elders are the most common victims of domestic violence by their adult children, making it rare for them to apply and seek help. Victims are often silenced by fear of revenge and institutionalization and by the desire to protect the perpetrators of violence from consequences. The most widespread form of abuse in Slovenia is financial or economic abuse. A third of abused elderly people suffered such abuse. A good quarter suffered from mental abuse, and a fifth of them were neglected. Violence against the elderly does not occur only in the family circle, but also in the institutional environment. This is an area of violence against the elderly, which is least explored, because it is difficult to obtain concrete data. In institutions, abuse occurs where staff are inadequately and poorly trained, where excessive workload, shortage of staff and occupational burnout occur. Any violence constitutes a grave violation of human rights and a decrease in the quality of life of the affected. In order to protect the elderly from violence, awareness and understanding of their rights are essential, while at the same time promoting their social inclusion. In order to reduce the risk of accidental abuse, those who care for older people must be protected from overload, while providing them with adequate knowledge and skills. The involvement of all professional services at local level, as well as cooperation between state institutions and voluntary organisations dealing with the older population, is crucial in addressing this problem. The Parliamentary Group of the Democratic Party of Pensioners of Slovenia will support the motion for a resolution because we believe that it is continuing its efforts to preserve the commitments and standards already adopted in the area of security policy of the Republic of Slovenia, identifying key areas of threat to crime, identifying the main problems in curbing crime and indicating solutions for its more effective prevention, treatment and limitation. We are confident that the resolution will help raise awareness of violence against older persons and prepare preventive programmes based on the establishment of an intergenerational co-existence key to solving the problem. Thank you.
